Three properties are load-bearing and tested rather than asserted: (1) **mutual by construction** — you announce to whom you marked and see whoever marked you; choosing someone cannot subscribe you to their presence (no server ⇒ nothing can compel their device to report), so `peer_marked_us` is carried and the UI reads "waiting for them to choose you back" instead of showing an unexplained grey dot, and choosing someone who already beaconed reveals it at once; (2) **soft state with a deadline** — a beacon claims "online for `ttl_secs`" measured from its *send* time and clamped to 30 min by the receiver, so a battery-dead/force-killed phone (which sends no goodbye) fades out on its own, a relay-backfilled replay is already spent, and a hostile peer cannot pin a permanent green dot; every read recomputes against the clock, and a heartbeat loop (180 s, TTL 480 s — ratio asserted at compile time) also sweeps lapsed claims into offline events; (3) **gated like every other control envelope** — accepted conversations only, so a stranger can neither push presence nor provoke the reply that would disclose ours. Only transitions raise events, so heartbeats never re-notify, and only the online edge notifies (being told a friend closed an app is noise). Storage adds an opt-in `Contact.comrade` flag (defaulted for pre-feature rows, preserved across alias edits — an alias edit must never silently un-disclose) and a `peer_presence` tree; `comrade_ui` adds `set_comrade`/`comrades`/`peer_presence`/`announce_presence`, a farewell beacon on `lock_vault` (snapshotting the comrade list *before* teardown and handing only the vault engine to the detached send, so the redb file lock is never held behind a slow relay), and presence columns on the chat list. **Verification**: `cargo fmt --check` / `clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` / `cargo test --workspace` all green, including 10 protocol cases, storage CRUD + the backward-compat read, 6 runtime cases (opt-in lifecycle, transition-only events, stale/out-of-order replay, stranger drop, silent record for an unchosen peer, aging out), and 2 two-peer integration tests over the in-process relay — comrades seeing each other come and go (including the answer-on-the-spot reply path) and a beacon reaching *only* the chosen peer, not another accepted contact. The Android lane (`./gradlew test`, JVM unit tests incl. new `PresenceMonitorTest`/`PresenceLabelTest`) was run in this environment against a locally installed SDK — a first for these entries, so the Kotlin half is compiled, not just reasoned about; instrumented/emulator coverage still belongs to `android-apk.yml`. `desktop/src-tauri` remains uncompilable here (no GTK/webkit2gtk), so its four commands are unverified until CI's desktop lane; the SPA half passes `node --check` and the existing `node --test` lane but has no UI coverage. Design record: `docs/PRESENCE.md`.
- **2026-07-29:** Frontend-framework question evaluated — see `docs/FRONTEND_STRATEGY.md`. An external proposal recommended consolidating the Compose and Tauri/JS frontends into a single Flutter codebase, justified by UI duplication. **Recommendation: do not migrate now**; this entry records an engineering analysis, not an owner decision. The premise does not hold against this repo's history: across all 149 commits, only **3** changed a screen on both platforms (`f048eb3`, `b31bbfa`, `d3c2cc6` — all 2026-07-11/12, none since), and the proposal's own examples were never built twice — `desktop/ui/` contains zero references to `journal` or `tara`, whose Tauri commands are registered and uncalled. The real problem is **desktop parity debt**, not double-authoring: desktop is missing journal, Tara, call history, and onboarding, and was last touched 2026-07-16 vs Android's 2026-07-28 (68 vs 30 commits in 60 days). Four defects make the plan unworkable as written: (**D1**) there are no "direct JNI bindings" to retain — `comrade_jni` is uniffi library-mode codegen (`lib.rs:133`, `build.gradle.kts:36-64`); (**D2**) adding `flutter_rust_bridge` alongside it means two `ComradeRuntime`s in one process, and `redb::Database::create` (`comrade_storage/src/lib.rs:115`) holds an exclusive lock, so the second vault open fails — while the alternative (services via `MethodChannel`) defeats the plan's own goal of reaching Rust without waking the Flutter engine; (**D3**, fatal) `CallScreen` cannot be rebuilt in Dart while `CallManager` is preserved, because it renders `org.webrtc.SurfaceViewRenderer` via `AndroidView` (`CallScreen.kt:590-627`) off a `StateFlow<VideoTrack?>` (`CallManager.kt:205-211`) — a native handle that cannot cross a channel, so the real choice is rewriting the repo's most-repaired file (2,039 LOC, carrying every 2026-07-15 fix above) onto `flutter_webrtc`, or leaving the call UI in Kotlin; (**D4/D5**) moving `crates/` breaks the root `[[bin]]`, `desktop/src-tauri`'s path dep, `comrade_py`'s maturin job, `build.gradle.kts`'s workspace-root resolution, all four workflows, and `deny.toml`, and `frb_codegen integrate` does not adopt a 7-crate workspace. It would also land underneath the owner's top-priority comms P0 (WP1–WP5 are desktop-JS) and swap the webview's mature WebRTC for community libwebrtc bindings. **Compose Multiplatform was never considered by the proposal and is the cheaper candidate here** — it keeps the 4,838 LOC of Compose, all 6,955 LOC of native services with zero channel plumbing, the uniffi bridge (JNA loads the same cdylib on desktop JVM, already built in CI), and `CallManager` untouched; its weakness is desktop WebRTC, which is the highest-risk item either way. Recommended instead: close the parity gap in the existing stacks (~1,200–1,800 LOC against already-registered commands), pull **WP15** forward so call decisions live in framework-free Rust before any frontend bet, and revisit if **iOS enters the roadmap** — the strongest argument for Flutter, and the one the proposal omits. **Verification honesty:** every repo claim is measured, with the reproducing command in the document's Appendix A; no Flutter/Dart toolchain was installed and nothing Flutter-related was compiled, so all framework claims are from published package metadata and are labelled as reasoned, not verified.
- **2026-07-29 (owner, supersedes the frontend recommendation above):** Owner read the analysis and directed the Flutter migration to proceed, explicitly setting cost aside ("if we ignore the effort"). Recorded as an owner decision, not an engineering one — `docs/FRONTEND_STRATEGY.md` §10 is the implementation record and its §§1–9 recommendation is no longer operative. The build followed the *corrected* plan (§8), not the original proposal, so D1/D4/D5 were designed around rather than hit. **D2 is resolved, not worked around**: `comrade_jni` now holds `static RUNTIME: OnceLock<Arc<RwLock<ComradeRuntime>>>` and **one cdylib exports both ABIs** — uniffi for the Kotlin services, flutter_rust_bridge for Dart. `nm -D --defined-only | sort | uniq -d` is empty (FRB 2.12 routes through a fixed PDE dispatcher by integer id, so collision is structurally impossible), verified on host, both Android ABIs, and inside the shipped APK (180 uniffi + 15 `frb_` symbols in one AArch64 `.so`). This is what lets `WakeWordService`/`RelayConnectionService` keep reaching Rust with no Flutter engine attached. uniffi's Kotlin codegen is unaffected — diffed against the pre-change commit, the only delta is one constructor checksum that moved because uniffi hashes docstrings into metadata. **D3 is decided and turned out sharper than written**: option (a) (rewrite `CallManager` onto `flutter_webrtc`) is not merely risky but *blocked* — call offers arrive on `pollEvent()` → `RelayConnectionService` → `CallManager.onIncomingSignal`, a foreground service that exists to run with no UI, so moving the `PeerConnection` into the Dart isolate leaves a detached-arrival offer nowhere to go. `CallManager` stays native; only a ~200-line `TextureVideoRenderer` leaf is added. Cost stated: Android-only, and **if iOS enters the roadmap option (a) becomes correct**. Porting also found a real latent bug — `MainActivity.kt:373-411` drove `Ringer.start/stop` and missed-call notifications from an Activity-scoped `LaunchedEffect`, so under Flutter the phone would only ring while an engine was attached; extracted to `CallStateReactor` on the process-lifetime scope. And it surfaced a pre-existing parity hole unrelated to Flutter: `sakha_status`/`pair_sakha`/`sakha_add_entry`/`sakha_read_ledger` exist on `ComradeRuntime` and are exposed by Tauri but by **neither** FFI ABI (only `sync_ledger` crosses) — that is why the couple sandbox is desktop-only; `test_turn_connectivity` has no Rust implementation at all. **Verified**: `cargo fmt`/`clippy -D warnings`/`test --workspace` (244 passed), `flutter analyze --fatal-infos` clean with no analysis excludes, `flutter test` 104 passed, a Linux desktop release bundle, a debug APK carrying both ABI slices of the Rust core, and a `dart:ffi` round trip proven **by mutation** (each assertion broken in turn to read the real value back, so it cannot pass for the wrong reason). **Not verified, and this is the load-bearing half**: nothing has been *run* on Android or a desktop window manager — no device, no emulator, no display — so `System.loadLibrary` never executed and no frame ever rendered; the vault has never been unlocked over the bridge, so only locked-state behaviour is exercised; no call has been placed from Flutter on any platform and desktop WebRTC (D8) is entirely unbuilt; stripped release `.so` size is unmeasured though the root `Cargo.toml` notes it sets `System.loadLibrary` startup cost. **Nothing was deleted**: `android/` and `desktop/` are untouched and remain the shipping frontends, their CI lanes still run, and the new Flutter lanes were added beside them — retiring them now would leave the artifacts users actually install untested on the strength of a frontend never run on real hardware. Retirement is gated on parity.

- **2026-07-30 (presence follow-up, from a field screenshot):** Owner ran the merged feature on two devices and reported the notification reading "⚲ is online / Someone you chose as a comrade is around", and the chat screen saying "Online now". Fixes: the notification body is now **"Your comrade is around"**, and the title's name is resolved with the app's own alias → published-handle → key precedence *at notify time* (`ChatEventRouter.peerLabel`) instead of trusting only the name the event carried — the same resolver now also titles **DM and media notifications**, which had been hardcoding `shortNpub(peer)` and so disagreed with every other surface about who someone is. The stray glyph was the small icon: `android.R.drawable.presence_online` is a legacy *colour* drawable, which the platform reduces to an unrecognisable mark when it tints a notification icon; replaced with a white-silhouette vector (`ic_notification_comrade.xml`, the same ★ the menu uses). On the Rust side `presence_display_name` now trims and drops whitespace-only names, so a blank can't reach a frontend as "a name". **Presence vocabulary is now Telegram's** (owner request): "online" (not "Online now"), then `last seen just now` / `N minutes ago` / `at 5:30 PM` / `yesterday at 5:30 PM` / `Monday at 5:30 PM` / `12 Jul`, with "last seen recently" for a comrade we have simply never caught online and "waiting for them to choose you back" kept for the un-reciprocated case (the one a status UI usually gets wrong). The rule is a pure, injectable-clock `lastSeenOf` in `DisplayName.kt` (10 new tests, CLDR-tolerant); wording stays in `strings.xml` with a plural; the clock follows the device's 12/24-hour setting; the desktop SPA mirrors the same ladder. Two real bugs found while doing it and fixed: a stale "X is online" notice could outlive the peer's presence (now cleared on the offline edge, plus `setTimeoutAfter(TTL)` so it self-expires if that event is ever missed), and `PresenceMonitor` grew from a bare online flag to `{online, lastSeenAt, peerMarkedUs}` so the conversation header can show last-seen without a store round-trip — advancing `lastSeenAt` **only** on sightings, never on an expiry edge (whose timestamp is up to a TTL later than the peer was actually there; overstating "last seen" is the one direction that lies). Header typography split so prose renders in the UI font and the npub tail stays monospace, and the long un-reciprocated label is omitted there rather than ellipsing the key off the end. **Verification**: `cargo fmt --check` / `clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` / `cargo test --workspace` green; `./gradlew test` green — 120 JVM unit tests including 10 new `LastSeenTest` cases and an extended `PresenceMonitorTest`; `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compiles. Desktop Rust remains uncompilable here (no GTK/webkit2gtk) and the SPA change is `node --check`-only, as before. Not fixed, flagged instead: turning off "stay connected in the background" stops `RelayConnectionService`, which is the *only* consumer of the native event queue — so live DMs/calls/presence stop arriving even in the foreground. That predates this feature and needs a COMMS-01-level decision (an in-app pump, or a setting that only affects backgrounding), not a quiet patch here.

- **2026-07-30 (COMMS-01 follow-up — the background-connectivity toggle was a delivery kill switch):** Found while shipping the presence follow-up above, then confirmed against the code and the setting's own copy. `RelayConnectionService` owned the *only* consumer of the native event queue (`ComradeCore.pollEvent`), and `start()` no-ops when **Settings → "Stay connected in the background"** is off — so turning that off stopped delivery **entirely, including with the app open**: no notifications, no live chat-list/presence updates, and **incoming calls never rang** (the call layer only ever sees signals through that loop). Nothing was lost — the Rust core still decrypted and persisted, so re-opening a conversation showed the messages — but the setting's summary promised the opposite in as many words ("turning this off means messages and calls only arrive while Comrade is open"). Default-on, so it only bit a user who deliberately traded the persistent notification away, i.e. exactly this app's audience. **Fix (owner picked option B of four):** split *draining* from *surviving backgrounding*. A new process-owned `EventPump` runs at most one drain loop, refcounted over `PumpHolder.FOREGROUND` (an Activity is visible — `MainActivity.onStart`/`onStop`) and `PumpHolder.SERVICE` (the foreground service, still gated on the preference), reusing the `MicHolderSet` shape already proven for overlapping mic holders. The service keeps its real job — a priority floor plus the ongoing notification Android requires — and loses the one it shouldn't have had. The single-consumer property that motivated COMMS-01 is preserved *structurally* (one loop regardless of holder count), so an Activity recreation still cannot duplicate listeners or notifications. Store seeding (cached feed, mesh status, comrade dots) moved out of the service's pump into `ChatEventRouter.seedFromStore()`, called on the vault-unlocked transition *and* by the service — so a user with background connectivity off gets seeded state too; it is idempotent (feed dedups by id, both monitors take whole snapshots) and every read is `runCatching`-guarded. Settings copy rewritten to describe what now actually happens. **Verification**: `./gradlew test` green — 125 JVM unit tests, including a new `EventPumpTest` (first-holder starts, one holder leaving doesn't stop it, an Activity alone keeps delivery alive with no service at all, duplicate acquire / unmatched release are harmless, stop-start restarts); debug + release Kotlin and `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compile. The lifecycle wiring itself (onStart/onStop ↔ service create/destroy) is the part a JVM test cannot reach — it is exercised by the emulator lanes in `android-apk.yml`, not here.

- **2026-07-30 (in-app update checks + the notification gaps around them):** Two asks in one pass. **(1) Updates.** Every merge to `main` cuts a release APK (`auto-release.yml`) and distribution is sideload/Obtainium (`RELEASING.md` §3) — so nothing in the app ever told a user that a release, security fix included, had shipped. A new `mullu.comrade.update` package holds the rule as a pure, host-JVM-tested object (`UpdateCheck`: semver parse/compare where a release outranks its own release candidate and an *unparseable* version is never treated as 0.0.0; GitHub release-JSON parsing that refuses drafts/pre-releases and refuses to *guess* between several APK assets; a once-a-day cadence that also fires when the clock has gone backwards; and a notify rule that announces a finding once, stays quiet about a skipped version, and speaks again for anything newer). `UpdateChecker` adds the Android half — one `HttpURLConnection` GET, a `StateFlow`, a `comrade_updates` channel, and SharedPreferences for the cadence/skip watermarks. It lives in the **preserved native tree**, so the Compose app and the Flutter host share one implementation (Flutter reaches it over a new `mullu.comrade/updates` channel; the shared file carries no Compose import, which is what makes `stagePreservedServices` keep it). **Two deliberate limits, both stated in the UI**: the app never downloads or installs an APK — "Get the update" opens the release page in a browser, so no `REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES` is held and Android's same-signature rule still gates the install (one-tap in-app upgrade is an owner call, not a side effect of adding a check); and the daily check discloses this device's IP to GitHub, which the settings card says in as many words, with a switch and a manual "Check now" behind it. Default-on, because a sideloaded privacy app whose users never hear about a fix is the worse failure. Desktop is deliberately out of scope: `release.yml` publishes an APK/AAB and nothing else, so there is no desktop artifact to update *to*. **(2) Notifications.** **WP11 (`docs/COMMS_ARCHITECTURE.md`) is done**: a tapped message notification now opens *that* conversation, a request opens the requests list, an update notice opens its card. Found while doing it: `Notifier.openAppIntent` built every `PendingIntent` with request code `0`, and `FLAG_UPDATE_CURRENT` mutates the *existing* PendingIntent when the code matches — so with two unread conversations in the shade, both taps went to whichever peer notified last. Request codes are now derived from the target. **Per-conversation mute** ships on both frontends (the ⋮ row #51 left out for want of a backend): `MutedChats`, a plain SharedPreferences set *outside* the vault because the notification path must decide before any unlock, consulted through a new pure `NotificationPolicy` that DMs, attachments and presence notices all now share — previously three inline `if`s that could disagree. Mute silences messages, attachments and the comrade-online notice; it deliberately has **no** rule for calls, so a muted chat still rings (Telegram's line too). Device-local, and the card says so. Also: message notifications now post a **group summary** so several unread chats collapse instead of stacking, pruned when its last child is cleared (Android drops a summary when children are *dismissed*, not when the app cancels them, and an orphaned summary reads as "you have a message" pointing at nothing). **Verification** (this session had a working Android SDK and Flutter toolchain, unlike the entries above; all figures are post-rebase onto `main` at fb4ce0b, so they include the media/screenshot work): `./gradlew test` green — **150 JVM unit tests**, 25 of them new here: 18 `UpdateCheckTest` cases (proven non-vacuous: they only pass because a real `org.json` is now on the *test* classpath, since the mockable `android.jar` stubs it to throw), 6 `NotificationPolicyTest` cases, and one more in a `ChatMenuTest` rewritten to sweep all four comrade×mute menu combinations. Debug **and release** Kotlin compile for the Compose app, and `:app:compileDebugKotlin` compiles for the Flutter host — which is what proves the shared-tree staging still picks the new files up. `flutter analyze --fatal-infos` clean, `dart format` clean, `flutter test` **220 passed**, 18 of them new here (`update_channel_test` 7, `settings_notifications_test` 9, plus two in `home_shell_test`); `node --test desktop/ui/*.test.mjs` 60 passed, unchanged — no Rust file was touched in this pass. The four FFI round-trip tests actually *ran* rather than skipping, because a host `libcomrade_jni.so` existed by then. **Not verified**: nothing has run on a device or emulator, so no notification has been tapped and no APK has been fetched by a browser; `flutter build apk` gets as far as `cargoNdkBuildDebugJniLibs` and stops (no NDK/cargo-ndk in this container), so the Flutter APK was not packaged; and no live call to api.github.com was made from the app — `fetchLatestRelease` is reasoned from the same `HttpURLConnection` shape `ModelInstaller.download` already uses, and only its callers' decisions are tested.

- **2026-07-29 (owner report: "offline msgs over same network isn't working"):** Correct, and it had never worked. `comrade_core::saathi` (libp2p mDNS + Gossipsub) had been in the tree for a long time carrying **nothing**: it only ran in the `OffGridTravel` workspace, and a workspace-wide grep found **zero** callers of `broadcast()`/`recv_message()` outside its own tests — the runtime used it purely as a peer counter for the connectivity indicator, which is exactly why the app could show a live mesh peer count while no message could ever travel over it. Fixed by giving the mesh the one thing it lacked, a payload: a second Gossipsub topic (`comrade/saathi/dak/v1`) carries **sealed `dak::Envelope` frames**, so the courier work's sealed unit — opaque ciphertext addressed only by a day-rotating `HMAC(recipient key, ctx‖UTC day)` tag — becomes bitchat's "flood it, only the addressee can open it" property on an IP radio instead of BLE. New `comrade_core::dak::mesh` (`MeshDm`, `seal_dm`, `open_dm`) plus `SaathiEngine::publish_sealed`/`recv_sealed`. Runtime: the mesh now starts on **unlock** rather than on workspace choice (`sync_saathi_lifecycle`'s condition became `workspace.mesh_active || vault_unlocked` — "the person I'm messaging is on this WiFi" is not a mode a user should have to select, while `OffGridTravel` keeps its meaning of mesh *replacing* relays); `send_dm_reply` and every `flush_outbox` retry seal onto the mesh when no relay accepts; a new ingress task opens frames addressed to us and feeds them through **`dispatch_incoming_dm` itself**, so gating, persistence, dedup, receipts and `/pay` detection cannot drift between transports; and a delivered receipt falls back to the mesh, without which a mesh-delivered message would be retried to its attempt cap because a receipt is what clears the outbox. **Cross-transport duplicates** are the subtle part: the mesh copy is keyed by the sender's local id and the relay copy by a relay-assigned event id, so id dedup cannot pair them — content dedup can, and the key includes the *transport* so that a second copy over the **other** route inside 2 minutes is dropped while "ok" typed twice (both copies over the same route) stays two messages. **Test-isolation fix, called out because it looks like weakening a test:** `two_engines_discover_each_other_via_mdns` asserted `peer_count() == 1`, which was only true while it was the process's only mesh test; mDNS discovery is machine-wide, so the new three-engine test legitimately raises the count (it failed with `left: 4`). Relaxed to `>= 1` — the strict form asserted a property of the test harness, not of the code. **Honest scope:** this needs a *shared IP network*. It is **not** Bluetooth, so two phones in a field with no router still cannot talk, and guest/hotel WiFi client isolation defeats it (the message stays queued, which is correct, but nothing is delivered). BLE remains unbuilt and is sized in `docs/OFFLINE_DELIVERY.md`; the `dak` layer is transport-agnostic precisely so BLE slots under it later. Verified: fmt, clippy `--workspace --all-targets -D warnings`, `cargo test --workspace` green, including a test that runs three real engines over real mDNS/Gossipsub in-process. **Not verified:** two physical devices on one WiFi, Android multicast/doze behaviour, and any router's client-isolation setting — that needs a real two-phone test.

- **2026-08-04 (two gaps found while building the profile plumbing, recorded rather than fixed):** **(a) `S-4` is an ID this ledger never defines.** Roughly a dozen code sites cite "AUDIT S-4" as the rule that decrypted media must not be left on disk — `android/.../ui/MediaAttachment.kt:143`, `desktop/ui/main.js:453`, `desktop/ui/handoff_transfer.mjs`, and the new `comrade_core::avatar` reasoning leans on it too. §3.1 defines `S4`, which is *plaintext contact keys* — a different subject. So every one of those citations points at nothing, and a reader who follows one lands on an unrelated finding. Not renumbered here because the fix is a judgement call (define `S-4` properly, or repoint a dozen comments at whichever row was meant) and doing it silently inside an unrelated change is how citations rot in the first place. **(b) The avatar URL policy does not close DNS rebinding.** `vet_avatar_url` refuses IP literals and suspicious hostnames, but a hostname that *resolves* to a private address defeats a host-string check, and so does a rebind between the check and the connection. Exit condition: resolve the name in `comrade_core::avatar` and pin the socket address via `reqwest::ClientBuilder::resolve`. Until then the attack needs control of a public DNS record, which is a smaller set than "anyone who can publish a Kind-0 event" but is not nobody — the module doc says so rather than implying SSRF is solved.

- **2026-08-04 (a profile page, and the two things that made it not a UI task):** Owner asked for a Telegram-shaped profile page on all three frontends. Two findings turned it into a core change first. **(1) A latent bug that only a profile page could expose.** `cache_pushed_peer_name` wrote a whole `PeerProfileRecord` with `about: None`, so a peer's profile-share envelope — which arrives exactly when a message request is accepted — erased any cached bio. Nothing read `about`, so nothing ever noticed; the moment a page renders one it becomes "their bio vanished when I accepted them", reproducible only by accepting a request. Fixed with `merge_peer_profile` as the tree's only writer, so a caller that learned a name can no longer claim there is no bio; regression test `a_profile_share_no_longer_erases_a_cached_bio` fails before the fix (verified by reverting). The accessor that was missing is `cached_peer_profile` — `about` had been persisted since bios were first cached and every reader went through `cached_peer_name`, which takes `.name` and drops the rest. **(2) A Kind-0 `picture` is an attacker-chosen URL.** Fetching it discloses the user's IP to a host the peer picked, and can reach the local network. `comrade_core::avatar` holds the policy, and deliberately as *pure functions* so the hostile-input suite runs in the default `cargo test` rather than only under `media-http` — a guard behind a feature flag is a guard nobody runs. Host parsing is delegated to `url` (already in the tree via nostr, no new dependency): `https://2130706433/`, `https://0x7f.0.0.1/`, `https://127.1/` and `https://[::ffff:127.0.0.1]/` are all loopback, and a string check misses every one. Also refused: private/link-local/unique-local/CGNAT ranges, `.local`/`.onion`/`.internal`, a bare hostname with no dot, and any URL carrying credentials. A byte cap does not stop an image bomb, so dimensions are read from the header without decoding — a 64 KB PNG can declare 65535×65535. **Remote pictures default to ON**, which is the owner's explicit choice between that and opt-in; the fetch is narrowed instead (accepted contacts only, never a stranger, never a blocked peer) and `set_remote_avatars_enabled` turns it off entirely. **Publishing is PNG-only**, because an avatar goes out unencrypted and public and a JPEG off a camera roll carries EXIF that routinely includes GPS — `strip_png_ancillary` is small and total where a JPEG APPn stripper is not, and is scoped out rather than half-done. `upload_public_blob` exists so the one public upload in the codebase is not spelled `upload_encrypted_blob` (which never encrypted anything); its doc states the three costs a user must be told — public, permanent in practice, correlatable with the npub. Verified: `cargo fmt`, `clippy -D warnings` on the workspace and on `comrade_core --features media-http`, `cargo test --workspace`, and `node --test desktop/ui/*.test.mjs` at 271 (up from 209). **Not verified, stated rather than implied:** `desktop/src-tauri` cannot even be compiled in the cloud sandbox (`gdk-sys` needs GTK), `flutter`/`dart` and the Android SDK are absent, and `flutter_rust_bridge_codegen` could not be re-run — the `frb_generated.rs` codec edits for the widened `ProfileDto`/`FoundProfileDto` are hand-written and the Dart half under `app/lib/src/rust/` has **not** been regenerated, so the Flutter lane will fail its codegen-staleness check until someone runs codegen. No profile page has been drawn on any frontend yet; this is the plumbing under one.

- **2026-07-30 (owner call — the key leaves the conversation header):** The header's second line used to read `online · npub1abc…wxyz`, on the rule recorded in the README that "the open-conversation header always shows the npub tail — handles are claims, keys are identity". That rule predates the ⋮ menu, which now carries **Copy key** and **Encryption info** (the key in full) one tap away, so the header was paying a permanent line of monospace for something reachable on demand. Owner asked for it to go. The line is now presence and nothing else — "online" / "last seen …" for a comrade, no second line at all for anyone else — which also retires `presenceHeaderText`, the variant that suppressed "waiting for them to choose you back" only because that label would have pushed the key off the end. README and `docs/PRESENCE.md` updated so the trust-model paragraph describes where the key actually lives rather than a header that no longer shows it. `./gradlew test` green (157 JVM tests); debug + release Kotlin and `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compile. No test covers header rendering — it is Compose UI, and the change is a deletion.
- **2026-07-31 (owner: download and install the update in-app, no browser):** Owner ran the shipped 0.0.49 against the newly-published 0.0.51, and asked for the model-download shape instead of the link-out: fetch the APK in the background, survive backgrounding, and *notify me to install when it's done* — without launching Chrome. That reverses the "it links, it does not install" limit the previous entry recorded as an owner decision, so `REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES` is now declared in **both** manifests. What the app does with it: **`UpdateDownloadService`** (a `dataSync` foreground service, deliberately shaped like `ModelDownloadService` — the pattern already proven here) streams the release's APK into `cacheDir/updates`, throttles progress to the notification bar every 256 KiB, and replaces the ongoing notification with a tappable *"Comrade 0.0.51 is ready to install"*; **`UpdateInstaller`** opens a `PackageInstaller` session and commits it, with `UpdateInstallReceiver` handling both the notification tap and the session's own `STATUS_*` callbacks — a receiver rather than an Activity precisely because the whole point is that the app can be closed while this happens. **The URL is not a parameter anywhere**: the service reads the release out of `UpdateChecker.status` itself, and `install` installs the file the service wrote, so nothing reachable by intent or channel can name an APK for Comrade to fetch or install. **Two gates, and the app owns only one.** `UpdateInstall.verify` (pure, 11 host-JVM tests) refuses a download whose package name, version code, or signing certificate disagrees with the running app — deleting it with a stated reason rather than feeding a system dialog that reports only "app not installed" — and it distinguishes *unknown* signers from *no* signers: `getPackageArchiveInfo` does not surface certificates on every device, so an unreadable archive is allowed through as `Ok(signatureChecked = false)` and logged as such, while an archive reporting zero signers is refused. A shared signer (not set equality) is enough, so a v3 key rotation is not refused. The other gate is Android's: the per-source "install unknown apps" grant, which cannot be requested in-app — `canInstall` reports it and the card links to the one system screen that grants it — plus the OS's own confirmation dialog on every install and its own same-signature refusal, which remains the guarantee that actually protects the upgrade. **Housekeeping that would otherwise rot**: a superseded APK is purged when a newer release is found (an "Install 0.0.50" button next to "0.0.51 is available" is a trap), a `Ready` state whose file the OS reaped self-heals back to offering the download, an `UpToDate` finding clears both the shade notice and the cache (that is what an installed update looks like), and a declined confirmation keeps the APK because the user may well tap Install again. Both frontends drive the same code: the Compose card grew a download → verify → install run of controls, and the Flutter card the same over `mullu.comrade/updates`, whose state channel now carries `{check, download}` in one snapshot. `openRelease` survives as the honest fallback for a release with no single identifiable APK, and for anyone who would rather not grant the install permission. **Verification** (figures post-rebase onto `main` at 4614d9f, so they include the screen-share and media-host work): `./gradlew test` green — **186 JVM unit tests**, 15 of them new here, covering the install verdicts and the download state machine; debug + release Kotlin and `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compile, and `:app:compileDebugKotlin` compiles for the Flutter host. `flutter analyze --fatal-infos` clean, `dart format` clean, `flutter test` **261 passed**, 10 of them new here — they drive the card through progress/cancel/install/permission-missing/failure states and pin that every download and install call crosses the channel with **no arguments at all**. **Not verified, and it is the load-bearing half**: no APK has been downloaded, verified or installed on a real device or emulator in this session — `PackageInstaller`, the `STATUS_PENDING_USER_ACTION` hand-off, the per-source permission screen, and the "ready to install" tap are reasoned from the platform contract and kept structurally close to `ModelDownloadService`/`CallActionReceiver`, but the first real proof will be the owner installing 0.0.52 from inside 0.0.51. `flutter build apk` still stops at `cargoNdkBuildDebugJniLibs` (no NDK here), so the Flutter APK was not packaged.

- **2026-07-31 (owner: "notify Alice when Bob types something in her chat and leaves it unsent"):** Shipped as a **nudge** — new `comrade_core::nudge`, a sibling of `presence` on the same gift-wrapped DM channel, carrying `{"comrade_nudge":1,"ttl_secs":480}` and nothing else. Alice sees **"Bob is online" / "Your comrade might need you"**, tapping through to his conversation. Recorded here because `docs/PRESENCE.md` §7 had explicitly ruled the neighbouring feature out ("typing indicators … leak considerably more about a person's day than around / not around"), so shipping this needed the line redrawn rather than quietly crossed: §7 now names the distinction and §6a is the design record. **The distinction that makes it defensible** is that a nudge inverts every axis of a typing indicator: once rather than continuously, *after* the draft is gone rather than while it exists, at most once per 30 minutes per comrade, to one peer who was deliberately chosen, carrying one bit — that something was written and not sent. There is no field for the draft, its length, how long it was typed for, how many times it was rewritten, or which of "cleared it" and "walked away" happened; a test asserts the JSON key set rather than trusting the comment, so adding such a field fails the build. **Five timing rules, all in one pure `draft_verdict`** so three frontends cannot drift: a draft that existed under 3 s is a stray keystroke and never a nudge; nothing is sent until it has stayed gone for 10 s, which is what makes the overwhelmingly common "clear the box and rewrite the same sentence" disclose *nothing*; a pending nudge older than the 480 s TTL is dropped rather than sent late, because sending stamps it `now` and would present an hour-old hesitation as this one — the dishonest direction, and one the receiver cannot catch; a second hesitation inside the cooldown is dropped rather than deferred (deferring would recreate the stale claim); and the peer-supplied TTL is clamped to 30 min exactly as a beacon's is. Sending **anything** cancels it — text, an attachment, or a message that only made it as far as the outbox — enforced in `RuntimeHandles::send_dm_reply`/`upload_and_send_media` rather than in three UIs, so no frontend can forget. The composer watch (`DraftWatch`) is **in memory only and cleared on `lock_vault`**: a draft abandoned in a previous process is not a promise still owed, and a nudge landing after the farewell beacon would claim the opposite of it. Honest consequence, stated in the doc: typing, clearing, and immediately force-killing the app sends nothing (backgrounding is fine — the connection service keeps the process alive, which is the "put the phone down and walk off" case the feature is actually for). **Receiving** is gated like every other control envelope (accepted conversations only, so a stranger cannot page anyone), then drops stale nudges by send time (the 2-day inbox backfill must not re-announce Tuesday), drops redelivered wrappers via the same `SeenSet` the call-signal path uses, and raises nothing for a peer *we* did not choose. Unlike a beacon it **writes no presence state at all** — no dot, no `last_seen_at`, no `peer_marked_us` — because a beacon's arrival already makes the mutual model discoverable and a second source of truth for those fields buys nothing. On Android it reuses the `comrade_presence` channel (someone who silenced that did not mean "except when it's urgent") **under the same notification id as the online notice**, so it replaces that line instead of stacking a second one about one person; it cannot silently downgrade back to "Your comrade is around" either, because the online notice only fires on a transition *into* online and the only path out of online routes through `clearComradeOnline` first. Mute silences it, an open thread suppresses it (`NotificationPolicy.shouldNotifyNudge`), and `setTimeoutAfter` drops it on its own. Both bridges expose `note_draft`/`abandon_draft` — the only two calls in either ABI that are **synchronous**, because a composer calls them on a keystroke and a `runBlocking`/async hop would cost more than the hash-map insert it was scheduling. The desktop SPA's share of the decision was extracted to a pure, tested `desktop/ui/draft_reports.mjs` rather than left inline, per this repo's own rule about `main.js` — the re-attribution when you switch conversations (this composer keeps its text, so what is left in it belongs to whoever is on screen next, while the peer being *left* is abandoned regardless of what the box holds) is exactly the "couple of lines in an event handler" that rule exists for, and it is easy to get backwards. **Verification, and this session had far more toolchain than the entries above — every lane CLAUDE.md lists as unavailable here was provisioned and run:** `cargo fmt --check` / `clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` / `cargo test --workspace` all green — **248 `comrade_core` unit tests** (23 new, covering the envelope shape, freshness, all five verdict rules and the watch's rewrite/send/cooldown/lock behaviour), **110 `comrade_ui`** (6 new: announced-once, replay dropped, stranger and unchosen-peer silence, junk-key/pre-unlock safety, lock drops a pending nudge), and **10 two-peer integration tests** over the in-process relay, 2 of them new and end-to-end — a message Bob writes and never sends reaches Alice as a nudge and never as a chat bubble, and one he *does* send never also arrives as a nudge. The `media-http` lane is green too. **`desktop/src-tauri` compiled here for the first time in this ledger** (`libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev` installed into the container): `cargo clippy --all-targets -D warnings` clean, so the two new Tauri commands are compiled rather than reasoned about. `node --test desktop/ui/*.test.mjs` **71 passed**, 7 of them the new `draft_reports` cases; `main.js` itself is `node --check`-only as always. **The Android SDK was installed too, so `./gradlew test` ran here**: **196 JVM unit tests** (post-rebase onto `main` at d1a9449, so the count includes the Tara/voice work), 0 failures, 1 of them new (`NotificationPolicyTest`'s nudge suppressors), and `compileReleaseKotlin` + `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` both compile — so `Notifier`, `ChatEventRouter`, `ComradeCore` and the `ChatsScreen` composer hooks are compiled, not guessed at. `:app:compileDebugKotlin` also succeeds for the **Flutter host**, which is what proves the staged shared-native tree (where the nudge notification actually comes from for that frontend) still picks the changes up. **Flutter ran here for the first time in this ledger** (3.44.8 / Dart 3.12.2, matching the CI pin): `flutter analyze --fatal-infos` and `dart format` clean, `flutter test` **266 passed** with 5 new, and `flutter_rust_bridge_codegen` was re-run rather than hand-edited — twice, confirming byte-reproducible output, which is exactly what CI's staleness gate checks. The four `rust_bridge_roundtrip_test` cases **ran instead of skipping** (a host `libcomrade_jni.so` was built for them) and now drive `noteDraft`/`abandonDraft` across the real ABI, which is what makes the "infallible even when locked" claim a tested one. **Not verified:** nothing has run on a device or an emulator, so no notification has been raised, tapped, or seen replacing an online notice in a real shade; the SPA has no UI-level coverage, as before; and the sweep's real-world latency (a nudge lands between `NUDGE_SETTLE_SECS` and one 60 s presence sweep later, deliberately — a courtesy signal does not deserve its own timer, and a later nudge is a more certain one) has only been observed against an injected clock, never a wall clock.

- **2026-08-01 (field report — "online" was claiming too much):** Owner saw a comrade shown as online while that app was closed but running in the background. Correct, per the rule this feature shipped with (`docs/PRESENCE.md` §4: "backgrounding deliberately does not announce offline — the connection service keeps delivering, so the user really is still reachable") — and wrong as a product: a phone in a pocket is reachable, but nobody is at it, which is exactly the small lie presence exists to avoid. **Online now means the app is open.** `ComradeRuntime` gains `presence_active` (an `Arc<AtomicBool>`, shared into `RuntimeHandles`); `announce_presence(online)` — the frontend's call — records it and sends, while the heartbeat's new `refresh_presence()` re-asserts *only while it holds*, which is the load-bearing half: without it the loop would undo every goodbye, announcing a backgrounded phone offline and then online again a heartbeat later. Android's `MainActivity.onStop` now announces `offline` (and `onStart` `online`, as before); a PiP video call keeps the Activity started, so a call still reads as online, which is true. Three details worth recording: choosing a comrade from a backgrounded app sends `offline` rather than `online` — they still learn we chose them, since any beacon proves reciprocity, without a claim we are at the phone; `lock_vault` clears the flag before the farewell so nothing re-announces in the window before the task is aborted; and `unlock_vault` sets it back, or a single lock/unlock cycle would silence the heartbeat for the rest of the process's life (found by writing the test, not by reading the code). Side benefit: a backgrounded app now sends no beacons at all, so the feature costs nothing while nobody is looking. **Verification**: `cargo fmt --check` / `clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` / `cargo test --workspace` green, including a new runtime test (default-active, the gate, lock clears it, unlock restores it) and an extended two-peer integration test — Bob backgrounds, Alice sees him go offline, Bob's heartbeat returns 0, and Alice never sees him come back until he actually opens the app. `./gradlew test` green; debug + release Kotlin and `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compile. Desktop is deliberately unchanged: a desktop window that is open *is* the app being open, and nothing there sets the flag, so the default (`true`) keeps its behaviour identical.
- **2026-08-01 (owner, three asks on the breathing screen):** *"Also send the your-comrade-might-need-you when someone clicks the one-minute button; rename the button to take a deep breath; add vibration feedback for breath in and breath out like the Pixel Watch."* All three shipped, and the first is the one worth recording. **The nudge gains a second trigger and not a second message.** Reaching for the breathing screen now raises *"your comrade might need you"* on the phones of the people the user chose — using the **identical `{"comrade_nudge":1,"ttl_secs":480}` envelope** an abandoned draft sends, through a single `deliver_nudges` send site so the two cannot drift apart. That is the whole design, not an economy: a comrade **cannot tell which trigger fired**, so a second reason disclosed nothing new about anyone, and the absence of a `reason` field is what guarantees it (the existing key-set test would fail if one appeared). The 30-minute cooldown is shared for the same reason and now lives in one pure `nudged_recently` used by both paths — a hard half-hour of writing, deleting and then stopping to breathe is one notification, not two. `DraftWatch` is renamed **`NudgeWatch`** to say so: the cooldown belongs to the person, not to whichever trigger happened to fire. The deliberate path skips dwell/settle/staleness because none of them have anything to answer when the trigger is a tap happening now. **The disclosure is stated where it happens.** The screen previously promised "No account of this is kept", and telling every comrade you used it is exactly the kind of thing a person would not expect from that sentence, so the note now reads *"No account of this is kept here, and your comrades are told only that you might need them"* — local storage is genuinely unchanged (nothing about having used it is written), and the wording is careful that what travels is "might need you", never "was breathing". Fires on **arrival**, not completion: someone who breathes for ten seconds and leaves needed them just as much. **Haptics** are shaped after the Pixel Watch's rather than a plain buzz — a swell rising through the in-breath, fading through the out-breath, silent through the hold, so the pace can be followed with the eyes shut and a single pulse never has to mean "a phase changed, guess which way". `BreathHaptics` keeps the waveform pure and JVM-tested for the two properties a real device would otherwise be first to catch: an amplitude outside the platform's 1..255 (`createWaveform` throws, on the phone, not in CI) and a ramp still running when the next phase begins. **A test caught a real inconsistency while being written** — the peak amplitude was 130 against a "stays gentle" bound of 255/2, so the constant moved to 120; the assertion was the considered number and the constant was the arbitrary one. Devices without amplitude control fall back to one-shots distinguished by *length*, because firing the same buzz for both halves is worse than not buzzing. Leaving the screen cancels anything mid-play, so a pocket never keeps humming. **Rename**: `breathe_title` is now *"Take a deep breath"*, and the feed's gentle-stop action — which opened the same screen under the near-duplicate label *"Take a breath"* — now points at the same string, so one destination has one name. Both bridges gained `nudge_comrades` (async, unlike the two keystroke-path calls beside it, because this one genuinely reaches a relay); the Tauri command is registered. **`app/` deliberately does not get it yet**: the attention pillar is Android-first and has no breathing screen there, and a disclosure with no UI to explain it is precisely what this feature must not be — so it is listed in `rust_comrade_repository.dart`'s existing "what the bridge carries that this interface does not" section, a decision rather than an oversight. **Verification** (every lane, on a container where Flutter 3.44.8 / Dart 3.12.2, the Android SDK and `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev` were all provisioned; counts are post-rebase onto `main` at 4d4a1ed, so they include the presence-activity and transport-precedence work): `cargo fmt --check` / `clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` / `cargo test --workspace` green — **268 `comrade_core`** (6 new: the deliberate trigger, both directions of the shared cooldown, a pause settling the draft it overtakes, and the no-comrades case remembering nothing) and **125 `comrade_ui`** (2 new: no comrades and pre-unlock are quiet zeros, and a pause claims the cooldown an abandoned draft would have used). `desktop/src-tauri` clippy clean. `./gradlew test` **239 JVM tests, 0 failures**, 6 of them the new `BreathHapticsTest`; `compileReleaseKotlin` and `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compile, and `:app:compileDebugKotlin` compiles for the Flutter host. `flutter analyze --fatal-infos` / `dart format` clean, `flutter test` **278 passed with zero skips**, and the bridge was regenerated by `flutter_rust_bridge_codegen` rather than hand-edited. **Not verified:** no device or emulator, so **nobody has felt the haptic** — its shape is reasoned from the platform contract and pinned by unit tests, but whether it *feels* like a breath is the one thing only a hand can answer; likewise no comrade's phone has actually lit up from a breathing session, and the OEM-vibrator fallback path has never executed.
- **2026-08-02 (owner, from the handset — the circle was already full):** *"It's starting with a big circle stating breathe in; instead it should start small and become big to signal inhale. Also the vibration feedback should match the size of the circle animation — start slow and gradually increase, and vice versa. Also show calming messages instead of the take-a-deep-breath text above the circle."* This is the previous entry's *"nobody has felt the haptic"* answering itself, with a screenshot, and it found a bug no unit test could reach. **`animateFloatAsState` starts *at* its first target.** With the opening phase targeting full scale, the screen appeared with the circle already inflated under the word "Breathe in", and the first thing it ever animated was the shrink — the inhale was being rendered as an exhale. There is no "from" to hand that helper, so the starting value has to be state the screen owns: an `Animatable` seeded at `MIN_SCALE`, with `LinearEasing` so the swell is constant-rate rather than the default ease-in-out. **The haptic now spans the phase instead of announcing it.** It was six 90ms steps — a 540ms swell at the *top* of each four-second phase, which says "a phase began": a thing you can only obey after the fact, and useless with your eyes shut, which is exactly when it is meant to be used. Now 20 steps stretched across the whole phase, so amplitude tracks the circle — faintest when it is smallest, strongest when it is fullest, and following one is following the other. `stepTimings` hands out the division remainder a millisecond at a time rather than dropping it, because that error repeats every phase and would be visibly ahead of the circle by the end of a sixty-second sit; a test pins the sum for both an even and an awkward phase length. The hold **snaps** to full rather than easing, so the one phase that is meant to be still is still. The no-amplitude-control fallback deliberately does *not* fill the phase: a flat four-second buzz at whatever amplitude the OEM picked is a phone that will not stop, not a swell, so it stays a short cue distinguished by length. **The line above the circle repeated the app bar's own title** — the most prominent slot on the screen spent on a sentence already read. It now carries five lines rotating once per full cycle, crossfaded; per *phase* would be one more thing to keep up with, on the one screen in the app with nothing to keep up with. Each line is a permission to stop, never an instruction and never a claim about what breathing will do — this app is not a therapist and says so elsewhere (`docs/TARA.md`), and a pause screen quietly dispensing advice would be walking that back. `breathingLineIndex` is pure and JVM-tested, including the empty-array case, and the call site uses `getOrNull`: the count comes from a resource a translation could ship empty, and this is the last screen in the app that should be able to crash. **Verification:** `./gradlew test` **256 JVM tests, 0 failures** — 9 new, taking `BreathHapticsTest` from 6 to 10 and adding a 5-case `BreathingScreenTest` — on a container that still had the Android SDK provisioned, so the Compose changes really did compile rather than being left to CI. Rust untouched. **Still not verified:** nobody has felt the *new* haptic either — a 20-step ramp across four seconds is reasoned from the platform contract and pinned by tests, but whether it reads as one continuous swell rather than a staircase is again something only a hand can answer.
- **2026-08-03 (owner, from the handset — it was a triangle, not a box):** *"Do we immediately breathe out and breathe in immediately? It feels forced, shouldn't there be some sort of hold? Also have a timer to set up a desired time, default to 1 min. The calming messages sound vague."* The first one is a **real bug that four separate pieces of prose were asserting away.** The cycle ran `(elapsed / PHASE_SECONDS) % 3` — in, hold, out, and then straight back into the next inhale with no pause on empty — while the KDoc said "box breathing", `docs/ATTENTION.md` said "a 60-second box-breathing screen", and the screen's own note enumerated "four counts in, four held, four out". That last string is the tell: it lists **three** counts and calls the result a box. Nobody reading either the code or the copy would catch it, because each one confirmed the other; it took someone sitting with it and noticing their body was being rushed. Three sides is a triangle, and forced is exactly what a missing pause feels like. Fixed by replacing the modular arithmetic with a four-value **`BreathPhase` enum** (`IN`, `HOLD_FULL`, `OUT`, `HOLD_EMPTY`) — an enum cannot be one side short without the type saying so, where `% 3` and `% 4` look equally plausible — plus a regression test that walks five cycles and asserts an inhale never follows an exhale directly, and another that every side is the same length, which is what makes it a box rather than any old four-part rhythm. Both holds map to the same silent haptic and the same "Hold" label: the circle is full for one and small for the other, so the word does not have to carry that, and a second word would suggest a second thing to do. **Duration is now the reader's** — chips for 1 / 2 / 3 / 5 minutes, defaulting to one, because the screen is reached for mid-something and the shortest useful pause is the one a person actually takes; the longer options exist so someone who came here deliberately need not keep re-opening it. Changing the length mid-sit keeps `elapsed`, so it extends or ends the sit in progress rather than restarting it — nobody asking for more time meant "start that minute again". The clock deliberately **keeps counting past the chosen length**: being done is a thing the button says, not a thing that freezes the circle mid-breath. That makes `breathingProgress` clamp at both ends, which is also what protects the progress bar when shortening the duration puts elapsed well past the new total. The bar itself carries **no digits** — a countdown would be a number to watch, the one thing this screen has avoided since it shipped — but a five-minute sit with no feedback at all is one you cannot tell you are two minutes into. **The calming lines were vague, and vague is the failure mode here**: an attention with nothing to rest on goes straight back to whatever it was chewing on. "You don't have to be doing something" and "this is enough, just this" gave it nothing; a jaw is somewhere to put it. All ten are now one concrete physical thing to notice or release — shoulders, the floor under your feet, the tongue on the roof of the mouth, the air being cooler going in than coming out. Ten of them, rotated every **two** cycles (32s) rather than every one, so the longest offered sit gets through the list without a repeat and a sentence is not one more thing to keep up with. One of the old lines said "for the next minute", which the duration chips would have made false — worth noting as the kind of copy that only becomes a lie when a nearby feature lands. **Defensive, and stated because it is unverifiable here:** the column gained `verticalScroll`, since the chips and the bar added ~50dp to a layout already holding a 200dp circle and a three-line note, and **nothing in CI navigates to this screen** — the emulator lanes only launch the app — so a short handset overflowing would ship unseen. **Verification:** `./gradlew test` **281 JVM tests, 0 failures** (`BreathingScreenTest` from 5 cases to 11), on a container that still had the Android SDK provisioned — so `verticalScroll`, the `BreathPhase` `when` exhaustiveness and `LinearProgressIndicator`'s lambda-progress overload all really compiled rather than being left to CI. Rust and both other frontends untouched. **Not verified:** nobody has breathed the four-sided cycle. Whether a four-count hold on empty is restful or feels like air hunger is a body's answer, not a test's — and if it is the latter, the honest fix is a shorter empty hold rather than deleting it again.
- **2026-08-03 (owner: "research whether two counts or four, and proceed"):** Asked at the end of the entry above, and the literature answers it: **two.** A hold after an exhale is not the mirror of a hold after an inhale, though a box treats them as equals. (1) It starts from a **smaller gas reservoir** — breath-hold tolerance is substantially shorter at functional residual capacity than at total lung capacity, because there is less stored gas and arterial CO₂ reaches the breaking point sooner. (2) The **brake is missing**: Hering–Breuer stretch-receptor inhibition of inspiratory drive is strongest immediately after an inhale and decays from there, and after an exhale there is none — so the urge to breathe arrives earliest in exactly this phase. (3) The **worst case is our user**: anxiety and panic are associated with heightened CO₂ sensitivity and the *shortest* voluntary breath-hold times, so the phase most likely to produce air hunger is the one the people opening a calming screen tolerate least. (4) **Slower is not automatically calmer** — vagal/HRV effects of paced breathing peak around 5.5–6 breaths/min; 4-4-4-4 is 3.75/min, below that band, and a 2025 head-to-head trial found box breathing produced significantly *higher* heart rate and significantly *higher* perceived exertion than 6/min, with the authors cautioning against prescribing it for downregulation. "Higher perceived exertion" is the instrumented version of *"it feels forced"* — the owner reported the finding before reading it. Corroborating: shortening the holds is the standard practitioner variation (4-2-4-2 is named as such; beginner guidance is 3-3-3-3 or 2-2-2-2), the one four-arm RCT running box breathing **individualises** its timing off a CO₂ tolerance test rather than fixing it at four, the Stanford RCT found exhale-emphasised cyclic sighing beat box breathing on mood and respiratory rate, and the most-used consumer implementation (Apple Watch *Breathe*) has **no holds at all** — inhale/exhale only, 4–10 breaths/min, user-settable. **So: 4-4-4-2, a 14-second cycle at ~4.3 breaths/min.** The consequence is that the sides are unequal, so **this is no longer box breathing and nothing may say it is** — the KDoc, `breathe_note`, `docs/ATTENTION.md` and the as-built table all stop claiming it, which is the same discipline that failed when three of them asserted a box over a triangle. `BreathPhase` now carries a per-phase `seconds`, `breathingPhase` walks cumulative boundaries instead of dividing (a single `/ phaseSeconds` is what the equal-sided version could get away with, and assuming it again is how both previous bugs happened), and the haptic ramp and the animation tween each read the length off the current phase rather than a shared constant. The empty pause gets **its own word** on screen — *Settle*, not a second "Hold" — because two phases of different length sharing a label leave the reader unable to tell which one they are in. Four properties are pinned rather than just the numbers: the four timings (as a decision, with the reasoning at the assertion), that the empty pause is **shorter than** the full one whatever the timings become, that the cycle stays inside 4–6 breaths/min so a future "make it slower to be calmer" trips a test, and the no-inhale-straight-after-exhale regression. An eleventh calming line was added because 28s per line × 10 would have repeated one in the last half-minute of a five-minute sit. **Verification:** `./gradlew test` **284 JVM tests, 0 failures** (`BreathingScreenTest` 11 → 14 cases), Android SDK provisioned locally. Rust and both other frontends untouched. **Not verified:** still nobody has breathed it. The evidence says a 2-count settle should feel like room rather than a demand, but the previous entry's *"only a hand can answer"* stands — and if it now feels too *loose*, the change to make is the inhale/exhale pair, not putting the four back on the phase that earns air hunger fastest.
- **2026-08-03 (owner states the purpose, and it changes an answer):** *"The purpose of this feature is to calm down users during time of anxiety, panic attacks and stress as well as breathing practice. Tailor the messages to be reaffirming given the usecases. Looks messages are switching after one full inhale-exhale — it's better to have one message for inhale and switch to another during exhale."* Two changes, and **the second one reverses a call recorded two entries above.** The lines used to change once every two cycles, on the stated reasoning that "a sentence changing every four seconds is one more thing to keep up with, on the one screen with nothing to keep up with". That was written for the attention-practice framing — the pillar's framing — and it is the wrong answer for the purpose the owner has now named. So the lines are **pairs** now: an inhale line to draw on, an exhale line to put down, `breathe_lines_in[i]` with `breathe_lines_out[i]`. The inhale half appears at the top of the breath and holds through the hold-on-full; the exhale half takes over as the out-breath starts and holds through the settle. The text turns over exactly twice a cycle, on the two phases that actually ask something of the reader, and **never mid-pause** — a new sentence arriving during a hold would land in the one moment there is nothing to do with it. `showsInhaleLine` and `breathingPairIndex` are the two pure functions; the pair advances on the inhale so a pair can never be split across a breath, which would show someone the wrong partner on the way out. **Repetition became acceptable in the same move, and that is the part worth recording.** Five minutes is 21 cycles against eight pairs, so the set comes round two and a half times. The previous entry asserted the opposite — "the same sentence coming round again reads as the screen having run out of things to say" — and had a test enforcing it. For someone waiting out a panic attack, a line they have already read is nearer a *mantra* than a shortage of content, so the never-repeat test is gone and `theSetRepeatingOnALongSitIsAllowed` stands in its place, specifically to stop a future reader "fixing" the repetition by citing a rule that was retired on purpose. **The register changed from grounding to reaffirming, and gained a hard limit.** The body cues stay on the exhale (letting go maps onto releasing tension), but the inhale lines now affirm — and specifically take the pressure *off*: *"you don't have to feel better yet"*, *"you don't have to do this well"*. Panicking about not being calm yet is a documented way for panic to feed itself, and a screen implying the reader was doing it wrong would be joining in. What the lines may **not** do is reassure about anything the app cannot know: no *"you are safe"*, no *"there is nothing wrong with you"*, no *"this is just anxiety"*. **Comrade has no idea whether these symptoms are a panic attack or a cardiac event, and a calming screen that talked someone out of seeking help would be the worst thing in this repository.** That limit is written into the `strings.xml` comment beside the arrays rather than left to whoever edits the copy next. What survives it is true either way: that they are here, that they reached for something, that nothing more is being asked of them this minute — plus the standing gates, no claim about what breathing achieves and nothing the reader can get wrong ("as much as is comfortable — no more"). One further check: no line instructs a depth or pace the animation is not showing, so nothing says "breathe out longer than in" over a 4-and-4 circle. The two arrays are paired by index and the caller takes `minOf` of their lengths, so a translation dropping one item truncates the set instead of desyncing the pairs or indexing past the end. **Verification:** `./gradlew test` **286 JVM tests, 0 failures** (`BreathingScreenTest` 14 → 16 cases), Android SDK provisioned locally. Rust, desktop and Flutter untouched. **Not verified:** nobody has read these lines mid-panic, which is the only test that matters for copy written for that moment; and the crossfade now runs twice as often, so whether it reads as a line arriving or as a flicker in peripheral vision is a thing to check on a handset.
- **2026-08-03 (field report — the install that never appeared):** Owner ran the shipped 0.0.65 against 0.0.66, downloaded the update in-app, tapped Install, and got an ANR (*"app isn't responding — wait / close"*) followed by **no confirmation dialog at all**; the card sat on "Waiting for Android to install it…", and tapping the "ready to install" notification did nothing either. This is exactly the half the previous entry recorded as unverified, and both defects were in the code, not the device. **(1) The APK was copied into the `PackageInstaller` session on whatever thread called `install()`** — the Compose click handler, the Flutter platform-channel handler, or `UpdateInstallReceiver.onReceive`, all of them the main thread. A release APK is tens of megabytes; that is an ANR from the settings card and, from the receiver, also a breach of the ten-second broadcast deadline. **(2) `STATUS_PENDING_USER_ACTION` was answered from a `BroadcastReceiver`.** The platform hands back an Intent for its own confirmation dialog and expects the app to start it, and a receiver is the one context that may not reliably do so: a notification whose `contentIntent` targets a receiver that then calls `startActivity()` is a **notification trampoline**, blocked outright since Android 12 (this app targets 34), and a background activity start from a receiver has been blocked since Android 10. Neither is an exception — `startActivity` returns normally and the dialog never appears — so the app had no way to know it had failed, which is why it claimed to be waiting indefinitely. **The fix**: `UpdateInstallReceiver` is deleted and replaced by **`UpdateInstallActivity`** — invisible (`Theme.Translucent.NoTitleBar`, no content view), `singleTop`, in its own `excludeFromRecents` task — which is now both the single entry point (settings button *and* notification tap, via `PendingIntent.getActivity`, so no trampoline) and the session's callback target, and which starts the confirmation dialog from `onResume`, never from `onCreate`/`onNewIntent`, because a relaunched activity runs `onNewIntent` before it is resumed and would land back in the background-start case. `UpdateInstaller.install` now returns immediately and does the verify-and-copy on a single-thread executor, abandoning any session a killed attempt left behind and setting `setAppPackageName`/`setSize` so a full device fails fast instead of part-way through the copy. **Three smaller things found on the way.** A missing `EXTRA_STATUS` used to default through to `0` — which *is* `STATUS_SUCCESS` — so a malformed callback would have reported an install that never ran; the new pure `UpdateInstall.outcomeOf` maps the sentinel to a failure and has a test that says so. `STATUS_FAILURE_STORAGE` now gets its own wording instead of the generic "Android could not install the update", because it is the one failure the user can act on. And a verified APK sitting in the cache was **forgotten on every process restart** — the state is in memory, the file is on disk — so the card asked for the same download again; `UpdateDownloads.reconcile` now picks it back up, size-checked against what the release published so a half-written file is not offered as ready. **One thing the app still cannot know**, stated rather than papered over: whether the system's confirmation dialog is genuinely up or was silently dropped. Both cards therefore offer *"Nothing happened? Try again"* under the waiting line, which walks the state back to `Ready` and starts over; a copy that is genuinely still running is left alone. Silent installs (`setRequireUserAction(USER_ACTION_NOT_REQUIRED)`, available on API 31+ for a self-update) were **deliberately not adopted** — the owner's report was that the prompt was *missing*, and swapping a sideloaded privacy app's binary with no confirmation is a bigger decision than a bug fix. **Verification**: `./gradlew test` green — **266 JVM unit tests**, 11 of them new here (`UpdateInstallTest` 11→15 for the session-status mapping including the missing-extra sentinel, `UpdateDownloadsTest` 4→11 for the reconcile and try-again rules); debug + release Kotlin and `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compile, and `:app:compileDebugKotlin` compiles for the Flutter host, which is what proves the new activity is picked up by `stagePreservedServices` for both hosts. `flutter analyze --fatal-infos` clean, `dart format` clean, `flutter test` **314 passed**, one of them new here — it pins that the waiting line comes with a way out and that the way out reaches the channel. Both hosts' *merged* manifests were checked, not just the sources: each carries `UpdateInstallActivity` with `${applicationId}` resolved and no trace of the receiver. **Not verified**: still nothing on a device — the fix is reasoned from the platform's documented restrictions and from the shape of the failure the owner reported, and the proof is the same as last time, one release later. If it misfires again the distinguishing evidence is *which* symptom survives: an ANR would mean the copy is still on the wrong thread, a silent no-op would mean the activity start is being dropped for a reason other than the two ruled out here.
- **2026-08-03 (owner: silent self-install, with a prompt to reopen):** Immediately after the fix above, and with the tradeoff already put to them: *"proceed with the implementation of silent self install and prompt to restart the app once done"*. So the session now asks for `SessionParams.setRequireUserAction(USER_ACTION_NOT_REQUIRED)` on API 31+, `UPDATE_PACKAGES_WITHOUT_USER_ACTION` (normal, no runtime prompt) is declared in **both** manifests, and `UpdateInstaller.notifyInstalled` posts *"Comrade 0.0.66 is installed — tap to open Comrade again"* on `STATUS_SUCCESS`. That notification is not decoration: applying an update replaces the process, so without it the app the user was looking at simply vanishes. **Asking is all an app can do.** Whether the dialog is actually skipped is the platform's call and varies by device, by OEM policy, and by which installer holds update ownership (Android 14+); when the answer is no the session returns `STATUS_PENDING_USER_ACTION` exactly as before. The confirmation path is therefore **live fallback, not legacy**, `UpdateInstallActivity` handles both without branching on which happened, and nothing in the code or the copy claims a dialog-less install. Reversing the previous entry's decision meant hunting down every place that asserted the opposite — `strings.xml`, the Flutter card, both manifest comments, `UpdateInstaller`/`UpdateChannel`/`update_channel.dart` doc comments, `README.md`, `RELEASING.md` §3, `PLATFORM_CHANNELS.md` §10 — because a comment promising a confirmation that may not appear is the exact failure mode this repo treats as a bug. **What is *not* given up**, stated plainly because it is the obvious worry: Android's refusal of an APK signed with a different key than the installed copy is unconditional enforcement, not a dialog, so it does not weaken when the dialog is skipped — and `UpdateInstall.verify` still runs first. What the user gives up is *seeing* the install happen, which the notification restores. `RELEASING.md` gained the release-manager consequence: a broken build now reaches users with less friction, so `versionCode` monotonicity and a green CI run carry more weight, and a mis-signed release fails as a silent "could not install" rather than a visible refusal. **One bug found while writing it**: `notifyInstalled` can be the first thing a *brand-new* process does — the session's `PendingIntent` resolves against whatever is installed when the platform sends it, which after a self-update is the new APK — and no service will have run `Notifier.ensureChannels`, so posting to `comrade_updates` would have been dropped in silence from Android 8, losing the only notice of the install. It ensures the channel itself now. **Verification**: `./gradlew test` green — **266 JVM unit tests**, unchanged from the entry above because this change adds no new decision: it is one platform flag and one notification, neither reachable from a host JVM test, and inventing a test for `sdkInt >= 31` would be ceremony rather than coverage. Debug + release Kotlin and `compileDebugAndroidTestKotlin` compile, `:app:compileDebugKotlin` compiles for the Flutter host, and both merged manifests carry the new permission. `lintDebug` reports no new issue on the changed files — in particular no `NewApi` for `setRequireUserAction`, which is guarded on `SDK_INT` directly so lint can see it (it still fails on five errors that predate this branch, in `CallService`, `UsageStatsReader` and generated uniffi code; lint is not a CI gate here). `flutter analyze --fatal-infos` clean, `dart format` clean, `flutter test` **314 passed**. **Not verified, and the honest state of it**: no install has run on a device, silent or otherwise, so whether *this* phone permits `USER_ACTION_NOT_REQUIRED` is unknown — and that is the one question the code deliberately cannot answer for itself. The observable difference on 0.0.67 will be which of two things happens: the app closing and an "installed" notification appearing (silent path), or the familiar system dialog (fallback path). Both are correct; only a third outcome — nothing at all — would mean the fix above is still incomplete.
- **2026-08-04 (owner: notify when a release lands, and stop the install happening out of sight):** Two asks, one on each end of the update path. **(1) The "new version" notification could only appear while the app was open.** `maybeCheck` ran from `MainActivity.onResume` and nowhere else, so the only person who ever saw the notice was someone already looking at the settings card that says the same thing — the notification was, in practice, decoration. `UpdateCheckJob` (platform `JobScheduler`, `setPeriodic(24 h, 1 h flex)`, `NETWORK_TYPE_ANY`, `setPersisted(true)` so it survives a reboot) now asks with the app closed and posts the notice itself, reconciled at every process start by `UpdateCheckJob.sync` from `ComradeApplication` — which serves both frontends, since `ComradeFlutterApplication` extends it. `JobScheduler` rather than WorkManager deliberately: WorkManager would be a new dependency (Room with it) for one periodic job, and on `minSdk 26` it delegates to exactly this. **Not push, and the doc comments say so** — there is no server and nothing is delivered to the device; it is the same GET on a schedule, which widens the disclosure `UpdateChecker`'s privacy paragraph already made in one specific way (once a day with the app closed, rather than when someone opens it), so switching auto-check off now takes the job out of the queue rather than merely stopping the reading of its answer. **Three traps on the way.** `JobScheduler.schedule()` on an already-queued id *restarts its period*, so scheduling unconditionally at process start would mean an app opened daily never reaches the end of a one-day period — the check would fire only for people who leave the app closed longer than the interval, which is backwards; `UpdateCheck.shouldScheduleCheckJob` is the pure guard and has a test named after the trap. The scheduler may fire up to the flex early, so a scheduled run gates on `SCHEDULED_CHECK_MIN_AGE_MS` (interval − flex) and not the full interval, or the run would be a no-op that pushes the next one a whole day out. And `Notifier.notifyUpdateAvailable` did not create its channel — fine while only a service ever called it, fatal once a process can exist *only* to run this job, because posting to a missing channel is dropped in silence from Android 8; it would have lost exactly the notice the job exists to deliver. **(2) The APK was copied into the installer session out of sight.** The 2026-08-03 fix moved that copy off the main thread, which cured the ANR but left tens of megabytes being copied behind an invisible `UpdateInstallActivity` that covered the app without drawing anything: no progress, no interaction, and the next visible event several seconds later. The copy now runs in **`UpdateInstallService`** (foreground, `dataSync`, matching `UpdateDownloadService` rather than inventing a `shortService` contract in the most bug-prone area of this codebase), reporting bytes into the shade and onto both cards; `UpdateInstaller.install` is replaced by a blocking `stageAndCommit` with an `onProgress` callback and a hand-rolled copy loop, since `copyTo` cannot say how far it has got. The activity now finishes the instant it hands over — the invisible window outliving its own work was most of what "I clicked install and the app stopped responding" actually was — which makes the session callback's `PendingIntent` relaunch the normal path rather than the exceptional one; if that launch is ever refused the symptom is the existing "Waiting for Android…" line with its *"Nothing happened?
